## novel view synthesis
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[SSA-3DGS: Unsupervised Removal of Screen-Space Artifacts for 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05598v3)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Novel View Synthesis (NVS) methods, such as 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS), rely on the assumption of clean, multi-view consistent, posed input images. Real-world captures can violate this assumption due to \textbf{screen-space artifacts}---static occlusions fixed to the 2D image plane rather than to the 3D world. Common examples include physical sensor defects, environmental obstructions (such as rain or mud on the lens enclosure), capture obstructions (such as a thumb over the camera sensor or a dashboard visible in dashcam footage), and digital overlays (such as watermarks or UI elements). When present, they are erroneously baked into the 3D geometry as ``floaters'' or near-camera artifacts, degrading the quality of novel-view rendering. In this work, we propose \textit{SSA-3DGS}, an unsupervised framework that jointly optimizes a 3D scene and a learnable 2D overlay to recover a clean 3D scene and the corrupting artifacts. By exploiting geometric consensus across views, our method effectively disentangles static artifacts from the 3D scene geometry without supervision or manual input. Across diverse synthetic corruptions and a self-captured real-world dataset, SSA-3DGS improves reconstruction fidelity by up to ${\sim}8$~dB PSNR over 3DGS trained on the same corrupted inputs, while faithfully preserving the corrupting artifact.</p></details> | Accepted at the IEEE/CVF Winter Conference on Applications of Computer Vision 2027 |
| **[Practical High-Fidelity Novel-View Synthesis of Mounted Lepidoptera](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31679v3)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Mounted butterflies are among the most striking objects in natural history collections. However, their beauty is notoriously hard to digitize in 3D: they are small and fragile, with microscopic hairs and vein structures. Capturing them in sufficient detail, therefore, requires a macro lens, which has a very limited Depth of Field (DoF). Moreover, a camera body cannot be maneuvered beneath a pinned specimen to photograph its ventral surface. We introduce an end-to-end pipeline that resolves these challenges, turning such specimens into photorealistic 3D models viewable from every direction. It combines three ingredients: handheld focus stacking for all-in-focus macro capture without a tripod, a non-contact first-surface mirror system that exposes the ventral surface without touching the specimen, and a segmentation-free, mirror-aware 3D Gaussian Splatting extension. We validate the reconstructions and design decisions on nine diverse specimens.</p></details> | Accepted at the IEEE/CVF Winter Conference on Applications of Computer Vision 2027 |
| **[QuantileGS: Quantile-Based Conformal Bounds on RGB Rendering Errors for Novel View Synthesis with 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21253v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Uncertainty quantification for novel view synthesis with 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) assesses rendering reliability at unseen viewpoints. This reliability depends on the discrepancy between rendered and true colors, which cannot be measured without ground-truth images. We therefore quantify uncertainty in this unknown discrepancy through upper bounds on per-pixel RGB mean absolute error. However, learned bounds may underestimate these errors and overstate rendering reliability, motivating calibration to a prescribed coverage level. In this paper, we propose QuantileGS, a post-hoc framework that combines learned rendering-error quantiles with scale-normalized conformal calibration to produce spatially adaptive error bounds. The framework comprises three phases. Phase 1, Rendering-Error Computation, measures RGB mean absolute error between observed images and predictions from a frozen 3DGS model. Phase 2, Ordered Gaussian Quantile Fields, learns two scalar attributes per Gaussian to predict lower and upper rendering-error quantiles. Phase 3, Scale-Normalized Conformal Calibration, calibrates the upper prediction on held-out images, using the quantile gap to scale the correction. Under the stated assumptions, the resulting bounds provide finite-sample marginal coverage. At a nominal coverage level of 90%, calibration raises mean empirical test coverage from 69.6% to 89.6% on LLFF and from 80.1% to 92.1% on Mip-NeRF 360. On LF, coverage remains conservative at 96.3% before and 96.4% after calibration. Applications to active view selection, Gaussian pruning, and RGB-D simultaneous localization and mapping illustrate how these bounds inform downstream decisions.</p></details> |  |
| **[TetraCoder: Adaptive Tetrahedral Encoding for Neural Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18267v2)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Implicit and explicit representations of geometry are central in computer graphics applications and come with their pros and cons. With surface sampling being faster on explicit representations and spatial queries being faster on implicit representations, finding efficient conversions between them is a highly desirable property. We present TetraCoder, a single resolution hash-free adaptive tetrahedral encoding that links an explicit tetrahedral spatial encoder with an underlying implicit signed distance field (SDF). The resulting hybrid implicit-explicit space is continuous piecewise affine (CPWA) by design, allowing to obtain an exact correspondence between the implicit SDF and the explicit analytic mesh. TetraCoder's design comes with additional perks, in particular: (a) it scales better than multi-resolution encoding methods due to its single resolution optimizable grid, (b) unlike hash-based methods, it is not affected by collisions, (c) it enables fast tetrahedral ray-tracing, geometry and appearance editing, directly through its adaptive tetrahedral scaffold. We demonstrate the versatility of TetraCoder by showing an improvement over state-of-the-art methods on multiple tasks: 2D/3D fitting from images and point clouds, 3D novel view synthesis, 3D reconstruction, and geometry/appearance editing.</p></details> | 8 pages, 4 figures |
| **[X-GS: An Extensible Framework for Perceiving and Thinking with 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.09632v6)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has emerged as a powerful technique for novel view synthesis, subsequently extending into numerous spatial AI applications. However, most existing 3DGS methods operate in isolation, focusing on specific domains. In this paper, we introduce X-GS, an extensible framework that integrates previously isolated 3DGS methods into the perception module of a VLM for spatial tasks, with two major components: the $\textit{Perceiver}$ and the $\textit{Thinker}$. The $\textit{Perceiver}$ performs online 3DGS-based SLAM with semantic distillation and outputs semantic Gaussians from unposed video streams. It leverages recent vision foundation models for stronger geometric priors, and we introduce three novel optimizations to improve semantic distillation efficiency. The $\textit{Thinker}$ interfaces diverse VLMs with these semantic Gaussians, unlocking spatial multimodal capabilities such as 3D visual grounding and scene captioning. Experimental results on diverse benchmarks demonstrate the efficiency and newly unlocked multimodal capabilities of the X-GS framework. Code is available at https://github.com/yueen-ma/X-GS.</p></details> | 16 pages, 5 figures. Accepted to Findings of EMNLP 2026 |
| **[GSPIM: A Temporal-Locality-Aware 4D Gaussian Splatting Accelerator with Polymorphic PIM and Multi-Frame Rendering Dataflow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04804v4)** | 2026-11-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>4D Gaussian Splatting (4DGS) enables high-quality real-time novel-view synthesis for dynamic scenes, supporting free-viewpoint video, mobile XR, and autonomous systems at the edge. Methods using explicit 4D distributions, keyframe interpolation, or anchor binding share two GPU data-movement bottlenecks. Temporal filtering follows model record loading, wasting LPDDR5 bandwidth on inactive data. Frame-isolated execution also overlooks highly overlapping active sets and depth relationships across adjacent frames, reloading shared attributes when working sets exceed on-chip cache capacity. To address these bottlenecks, we present GSPIM, a temporal-locality-aware 4DGS accelerator for PIM-enabled edge devices. Polymorphic PIM adapts a shared near-memory datapath to representation-specific field layouts and selection procedures. It filters and compacts valid data into contiguous inputs before the GPU accesses complete attributes, reducing ineffective transfers. Multi-Frame Rendering Dataflow reuses active attributes and stable depth relationships within a temporal window, reducing repeated work across frames. Hybrid Dataflow Scheduling overlaps near-memory data preparation with GPU rendering to reduce waiting. Evaluation combines a GPU-PIM model calibrated to Jetson AGX Orin 64 GB with a 1z DRAM physical implementation of the added circuits. Across three 4DGS methods and six dynamic scenes, GSPIM achieves a 4.15x geometric-mean speedup and a 4.99x geometric-mean energy-efficiency improvement over a fully optimized frame-by-frame GPU baseline. The maximum scene-level PSNR difference is 0.011 dB, and the added physical footprint is 18.7172 square millimeters across 32 LPDDR5 dies.</p></details> | 17 pages, 23 figures, Accepted by HPCA 2027. Code repo: https://github.com/MAdrid1011/GSPIM |

| **[ReTouchGS: Ultralight Render-free Aesthetic Color Enhancement and Transfer for 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23054v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) enables photorealistic scene reconstruction. Averaged over 11 scenes on a workstation, Cross3DGS has higher quality than VkSplat with no significant difference in training time, and Brush reports about three times the training time at comparable quality. | **[Accelerating 3D Gaussian Splatting Training through Selective Freezing and Computation Caching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22059v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) becomes a key primitive for immersive 3D experiences. Project page: https://jhxcho.github.io/voda/ |
| **[QuantileGS: Quantile-Based Conformal Bounds on RGB Rendering Errors for Novel View Synthesis with 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21253v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Uncertainty quantification for novel view synthesis with 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) assesses rendering reliability at unseen viewpoints. Phase 2, Ordered Gaussian Quantile Fields, learns two scalar attributes per Gaussian to predict lower and upper rendering-error quantiles. At a nominal coverage level of 90%, calibration raises mean empirical test coverage from 69.6% to 89.6% on LLFF and from 80.1% to 92.1% on Mip-NeRF 360. | **[GC-MoE on SM120: Overlapping Expert-Parallel Token Dispatch with Warp-Level FP8xFP4 Block-Scaled MMA on Consumer Blackwell](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16219v1)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Expert-parallel Mixture-of-Experts decoding is latency bound: each layer serializes a token exchange, a grouped GEMM over routed experts, and a combine, while only a few tokens per rank are in flight. | **[Instant NuRec: Feed-Forward 3D Gaussian Reconstruction for Driving Scene Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14203v2)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D simulation platforms are critical for autonomous driving because they enable end-to-end policy evaluation, thereby reducing development costs and improving safety. In this work, we present Instant NuRec, a feed-forward neural reconstruction model that turns a short multi-view driving log into a fully simulatable 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) world in a single forward pass. The model accepts multi-view input from a calibrated camera rig and emits a layered output consisting of static and dynamic 3DGS layers, a sky cubemap, and per-camera ISP corrections, while providing native support for non-pinhole camera models via 3DGUT. It reconstructs a 10-20-second multi-camera scene in roughly 1.5 seconds and achieves a PSNR on the Waymo Open Dataset that is 2.01 dB above the strongest evaluated baseline. Instant NuRec is deeply integrated into NuRec and is compatible with AlpaSim for closed-loop simulation.</p></details> | Project Page: https://research.nvidia.com/labs/sil/projects/instant-nurec/ |
| **[SplatLab: Autonomous Scientific Discovery for 3DGS Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30468v3)** | 2026-11-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scientific discovery requires more than generating hypotheses: ideas must be grounded in prior work, translated into executable methods, experimentally validated, challenged by counterevidence, and communicated through reproducible findings. Despite recent advances in autonomous research agents, closing this entire loop remains challenging in rapidly evolving computer-vision fields with complex training pipelines and expensive evaluations. We introduce SplatLab, an autonomous scientific discovery system for 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) that integrates literature understanding, hypothesis generation, implementation, experimental refinement, falsification, and manuscript writing into a unified research pipeline. Given a research prompt, a target-paper URL, or no input at all, SplatLab identifies research opportunities, proposes novel methods, trains and ablates candidates across Mip-NeRF 360 and LLFF, and produces manuscripts grounded in verifiable experimental evidence. Unlike prior systems, SplatLab performs discovery over verified baseline implementations using domain-aligned modification scaffolds. A cascade-weighted Elo tournament evolves candidate methods using measured GPU performance and verbal-gradient feedback, while a standing falsifier incorporates adversarial stress testing directly into selection. An integrity audit binds reported claims to logged experiments, and reviewer-driven feedback can initiate further experimentation rather than merely revising text. SplatLab discovers methods achieving PSNR improvements of +0.18 to +3.78 dB over matched baselines, including +3.78 dB on astrophotographic nebula rendering, a previously unexplored setting for 3DGS. By closing the loop between scientific reasoning, executable experimentation, and evidence-backed communication, SplatLab compresses months of researcher effort into days of mostly autonomous compute.</p></details> | Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. 14 pages, 4 figures. Project page: https://seemandhar.github.io/splatlab |

## 3DGS
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[ReTouchGS: Ultralight Render-free Aesthetic Color Enhancement and Transfer for 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23054v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) enables photorealistic scene reconstruction. Existing 3DGS color transfer and interactive editing methods are guided by reference images and user-specified color adjustments. However, in many applications, users simply want aesthetically guided color enhancement that automatically makes the scene more visually appealing while maintaining consistent colors and tones across viewpoints. We present ReTouchGS, to our knowledge the first framework for reference-free aesthetic color enhancement of pretrained 3DGS models. It predicts a scene-level color lookup table (LUT) from the model and its associated camera parameters. ReTouchGS also supports reference-guided color transfer by fitting a scene-level LUT using one edited view of the same scene and its corresponding original render. A shared blend-aware color refinement network with only 44.7K parameters refines an analytic color update to realize either color mapping without rendering. It preserves geometry and delivers standard 3DGS assets in seconds. We also design the ReTouch3D benchmark to support the evaluation of aesthetic color enhancement and reference-guided color transfer across viewpoints. Experiments demonstrate high color accuracy and strong generalization to unseen scenes in both aesthetic color enhancement and reference-guided color transfer.</p></details> |  |
| **[SSA-3DGS: Unsupervised Removal of Screen-Space Artifacts for 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05598v3)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Novel View Synthesis (NVS) methods, such as 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS), rely on the assumption of clean, multi-view consistent, posed input images. Real-world captures can violate this assumption due to \textbf{screen-space artifacts}---static occlusions fixed to the 2D image plane rather than to the 3D world. Common examples include physical sensor defects, environmental obstructions (such as rain or mud on the lens enclosure), capture obstructions (such as a thumb over the camera sensor or a dashboard visible in dashcam footage), and digital overlays (such as watermarks or UI elements). When present, they are erroneously baked into the 3D geometry as ``floaters'' or near-camera artifacts, degrading the quality of novel-view rendering. In this work, we propose \textit{SSA-3DGS}, an unsupervised framework that jointly optimizes a 3D scene and a learnable 2D overlay to recover a clean 3D scene and the corrupting artifacts. By exploiting geometric consensus across views, our method effectively disentangles static artifacts from the 3D scene geometry without supervision or manual input. Across diverse synthetic corruptions and a self-captured real-world dataset, SSA-3DGS improves reconstruction fidelity by up to ${\sim}8$~dB PSNR over 3DGS trained on the same corrupted inputs, while faithfully preserving the corrupting artifact.</p></details> | Accepted at the IEEE/CVF Winter Conference on Applications of Computer Vision 2027 |
| **[ReScene: Incremental Scene Chronology from Sparse Revisits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20433v2)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scene chronology aims to reconstruct a scene across visits separated by hours or days, during which objects may change in position, presence or shape. Such reconstruction must retain every earlier state, yet each revisit often provides only a few images. Storing a complete model for each visit duplicates the unchanged scene, whereas updating a single shared model alters earlier states. We present ReScene, which stores each visit as an immutable compact update, so that any earlier visit can be recovered exactly by applying the stored updates in order, without retaining past images. To localise each update, ReScene attributes the disagreement between the preceding reconstruction and the new images to individual Gaussians through their compositing weights. This attribution guides the suppression of outdated Gaussians and the refinement of persistent ones, while newly visible surfaces are initialised from learned multi-view depth. We also introduce Multi-State, a benchmark of non-rigid deformation and partial material removal, including synthetic scenes with ground-truth geometry. ReScene achieves quality higher than sequential 3DGS checkpointing with 2.5--4.8$\times$ less uncompressed history storage. At comparable PSNR, it also reduces foreground depth error by 3.2$\times$ relative to sequential ChronoGS, showing that accurate rendering does not necessarily imply accurate geometry. Code and benchmark are available at https://waychin-weiqin.github.io/ReScene/.</p></details> | 22 pages |
| **[AbacusGS: Gaussian Splatting without Per-Splat Sorting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01534v2)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D Gaussian Splatting sorts every splat by depth in every frame. Viewers that draw splats with the hardware rasterizer and sort them on the CPU, as most web and mobile viewers do, spend most of the frame time in this sort. We present AbacusGS, which stores the blending order of a 3DGS scene in the scene layout. AbacusGS groups the splats into octree boxes of at most 128 splats and stores each box in order along an axis chosen at write time. In each frame, AbacusGS sorts only the visible boxes, and one dot product per box decides whether the box is read forward or backward. A short order-aware fine-tuning converts an existing 3DGS model, and the over operator of 3DGS stays unchanged. On 13 standard scenes, AbacusGS loses 0.13 dB PSNR on average against an equally fine-tuned 3DGS model. In an OpenGL viewer that orders splats on the CPU, AbacusGS renders frames 4.5 times faster than per-splat sorting. We also present a compact format of 17 bytes per splat and a CUDA tile renderer that use the same order. The code will be released.</p></details> | The authors request withdrawal of this preprint. The manuscript has been substantially revised for submission to a conference, and this early version no longer reflects the scope and content of the current work |
| **[Cross3DGS: Unified Vulkan-Based 3D Gaussian Splatting Training from Workstations to Mobile Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22240v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scenes captured with handheld and wearable cameras are still shipped to a CUDA workstation for 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) training. Training on the device that captured them, or on its companion phone or laptop, requires more than translating kernels: GPUs and drivers differ in parallel features, memory limits, and command submission, and these differences decide whether a training schedule executes and returns the correct update. We present Cross3DGS, a cross-vendor, cross-platform Vulkan trainer that keeps one optimizer and chooses its execution schedule per device in two steps: rules on device limits, workload size, and observed driver failures exclude the schedules known not to run correctly, and a fixed preference order selects one of the remaining feasible schedules. The same core completes standard-quality training (30k updates, one million Gaussians) on every configuration we measured, spanning NVIDIA, Intel, Arm, Qualcomm, AMD, and Apple GPUs and including Android devices and an iPhone, whereas the existing portable trainers VkSplat and Brush do not run on some of the mobile GPUs. Averaged over 11 scenes on a workstation, Cross3DGS has higher quality than VkSplat with no significant difference in training time, and Brush reports about three times the training time at comparable quality. The final quality of Cross3DGS is nearly identical across devices. A mobile application turns a handheld capture into a viewable model entirely on the device.</p></details> | Accepted to the WACV 2027 Workshop on Eyes of the Future (EOF). 19 pages including appendix |
| **[Accelerating 3D Gaussian Splatting Training through Selective Freezing and Computation Caching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22059v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) becomes a key primitive for immersive 3D experiences. However, its applicability is limited by its long training overhead. Although prior work has shown that gradient accumulation is the dominant bottleneck in training, existing works either have limited speedup or degrade reconstruction quality. To address this issue, we present ElsaGS, an algorithm-hardware co-designed framework that accelerates 3DGS training with negligible accuracy degradation. Our key observation is that most Gaussians converge early, while later iterations only refine a small subset of Gaussians. Thus, we propose a technique, progressive Gaussian freezing, which dynamically freezes well-converged Gaussians and caches their cumulative contributions, thereby reducing computations in both forward rasterization and backward gradient accumulation. To make caching efficient, we further propose an architecture, ElsaGS core, which integrates a pixel-parallel rasterization array with a set-associative frozen Gaussian cache, FGcache. Across three 3DGS datasets, ElsaGS freezes up to 90% of Gaussians with negligible quality loss and achieves up to 20.6x end-to-end speedup and 99.4% energy reduction over GPUs.</p></details> |  |
| **[QuantileGS: Quantile-Based Conformal Bounds on RGB Rendering Errors for Novel View Synthesis with 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21253v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Uncertainty quantification for novel view synthesis with 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) assesses rendering reliability at unseen viewpoints. We therefore quantify uncertainty in this unknown discrepancy through upper bounds on per-pixel RGB mean absolute error. However, learned bounds may underestimate these errors and overstate rendering reliability, motivating calibration to a prescribed coverage level. In this paper, we propose QuantileGS, a post-hoc framework that combines learned rendering-error quantiles with scale-normalized conformal calibration to produce spatially adaptive error bounds. The framework comprises three phases. Phase 1, Rendering-Error Computation, measures RGB mean absolute error between observed images and predictions from a frozen 3DGS model. Phase 2, Ordered Gaussian Quantile Fields, learns two scalar attributes per Gaussian to predict lower and upper rendering-error quantiles. Phase 3, Scale-Normalized Conformal Calibration, calibrates the upper prediction on held-out images, using the quantile gap to scale the correction. Under the stated assumptions, the resulting bounds provide finite-sample marginal coverage. At a nominal coverage level of 90%, calibration raises mean empirical test coverage from 69.6% to 89.6% on LLFF and from 80.1% to 92.1% on Mip-NeRF 360. On LF, coverage remains conservative at 96.3% before and 96.4% after calibration. Applications to active view selection, Gaussian pruning, and RGB-D simultaneous localization and mapping illustrate how these bounds inform downstream decisions.</p></details> |  |
| **[X-GS: An Extensible Framework for Perceiving and Thinking with 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.09632v6)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has emerged as a powerful technique for novel view synthesis, subsequently extending into numerous spatial AI applications. | **[A Gaussian Primitive Data Management Framework for Efficient 3DGS Trajectory Rendering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20546v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Trajectory rendering, which renders novel views of a reconstructed 3D scene along a camera path, is a core operation in digital twins and autonomous inspection. We treat this as a spatial data management problem, where anchors are spatial objects, views are range queries, and decoded Gaussians are shared derived data, and propose GDM-GS, which removes redundancy at three levels. Within a view, an anchor index and an occluder index answer each view as a holed-frustum range query, so out-of-view and provably hidden anchors are never decoded. Across views, shared materialization decodes each requested anchor once per group of consecutive views while preserving each view's selection. Across processors, a hybrid pipeline balances selections between the CPU and the GPU before grouped decoding under bounded memory. Experiments on large-scale public scenes show that GDM-GS substantially reduces trajectory rendering latency while preserving image quality.</p></details> |  |
| **[TRUCO: A Scalable Lock-Free Algorithm for Parallel Contour Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37297v3)** | 2026-11-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Extracting binary contours and their containment hierarchy is a fundamental step in many computer vision pipelines. TRUCO relies on two core ideas. First, the image is partitioned into horizontal stripes, and a geometric ownership rule allows each thread to trace complete contours independently across stripe boundaries, avoiding both locks and post-processing merges. Across diverse high-resolution datasets-from sparse segmentation masks to dense real-world photographs-TRUCO achieves speedups of 4.7x to 17.4x over the fastest sequential baseline with hierarchy computation, and 2.3x to 17.8x without it.</p></details> |  |
| **[OctreeSplat: Hierarchical Octree-Guided Feed-Forward Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02658v2)** | 2026-11-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) methods typically regress pixel-aligned Gaussian primitives, often causing excessive overlap and artifacts, while inaccuracies in predicted camera poses can lead to misalignment in novel-view synthesis (NVS). | **[SemLite-GS: Semantic-Selective Parallel Gaussian Splatting for Lightweight Building Reconstruction from UAV Oblique Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19773v1)** | 2026-11-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Lightweight 3D building models support urban spatial analysis, but their production often requires dense scene reconstruction, building extraction, and mesh simplification. | **[PRG-Fusion: Orchestrating Generative Priors with Reconstruction Evidence for Driving View Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06948v2)** | 2026-11-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Synthesizing photorealistic driving videos along specified trajectories is essential for scalable closed-loop simulation. Our key insight is that reconstruction reliability and available support should jointly determine how previously unseen regions are generated and reliable content is preserved. We derive pixel-wise Preserve, Repair, and Generate (PRG) labels from reconstruction evidence to distinguish reliable appearance, degraded but supported content, and regions with insufficient support. At inference, these labels serve as a unified routing policy for region-aware spatiotemporal synthesis, orchestrating 3DGS appearance preservation, LiDAR-guided structural correction, and video-prior-driven content completion across Preserve, Repair, and Generate regions, respectively. We then follow a two-stage training paradigm, first establishing geometric control from sparse LiDAR projections and subsequently learning appearance control from dense 3DGS renderings. Extensive experiments on Waymo demonstrate that \mbox{\textbf{PRG-Fusion}} achieves state-of-the-art overall performance in novel trajectory video synthesis, with superior visual quality and geometric fidelity while maintaining competitive view consistency under large trajectory shifts. | **[MACRO: Training-free Multi-plane Attention for Closeup Render Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03875v2)** | 2026-11-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Close-up rendering, zooming into a scene well beyond any training camera, is important for virtual production and interactive 3D content, yet remains an open challenge. 3D Gaussian splatting (3DGS) enables high-fidelity, real-time novel view synthesis, but its rendering quality degrades at close range. MACRO resolves the scale gap by leveraging the scene's known 3D structure: it decomposes the close-up into depth planes, crops and resizes references in image space to match the scale of each plane before encoding, and applies a depth-aware attention mask so each token attends only to scale-matched references. The method requires no architectural changes or additional training. We further contribute two new close-up novel view synthesis benchmarks, the first standardized evaluation protocol for this setting, and demonstrate state-of-the-art results on both, outperforming existing 3DGS and diffusion-based methods on both reconstruction and perceptual metrics. | **[Stage-wise Lineage-aware Dropout for Sparse-View 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17752v1)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sparse-view 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) is prone to overfitting because only a limited number of images are available to constrain scene geometry. | **[GC-MoE on SM120: Overlapping Expert-Parallel Token Dispatch with Warp-Level FP8xFP4 Block-Scaled MMA on Consumer Blackwell](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16219v1)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Expert-parallel Mixture-of-Experts decoding is latency bound: each layer serializes a token exchange, a grouped GEMM over routed experts, and a combine, while only a few tokens per rank are in flight. SM120 exposes block-scaled tensor cores only through warp-level mma.sync.aligned.kind::mxf8f6f4: no tensor memory, no two-CTA MMA, no cluster multicast. We rebuild the kernel around a swap-AB warp-level block-scaled MMA with FP4 weights in the register A operand and FP8 activations in B, plus SM120-specific mechanisms: scale-byte selection through MMA immediates, a compact-weights path, an adaptive token-tile geometry, an expert-granular streaming dispatch with a batched bulk-pull engine and a 64-bit deduplicating pull cache, full-width BF16 returns drained by resident relay copiers over a position-major arena, and shared experts folded into the compute role. ## Gaussian Splatting
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

Without fine-tuning, data-free LILA surpasses PruneNet (45M-parameter RL policy) by up to 2.12~pp in zero-shot accuracy on LLaMA-2-7B and outperforms WikiText-2-calibrated SliceGPT by up to 6.1~pp across all sparsity levels, confirming data-free performance is not an artifact of omitting activation statistics. After one LoRA RFT epoch (matched WikiText-2 protocol), LILA Act surpasses SliceGPT+RFT by 6.39~pp on average using 98.4\% fewer calibration samples (16 vs.\ 1,024) across LLaMA-2-7B and Phi-2. NTK analysis confirms a $17{\times}$ reduction in functional distortion versus random pruning, providing theoretical grounding for the spectral criterion.</p></details> | 31 Pages |
| **[BLaFA: Bridging Language to 3D Functional Affordances for Dexterous Robotic Grasping in Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08410v4)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In industrial assembly, equipment maintenance, and everyday tool-use scenarios, robots need not only to grasp objects stably but also to establish task-appropriate contact configurations according to task intentions and tool functionality. However, existing methods lack explicit mappings from open-vocabulary instructions to 3D functional contact structures and dexterous grasp configurations, limiting robots' adaptability to diverse tools and functional tasks. To address this limitation, we propose BLaFA (Bridging Language to 3D Functional Affordances for Dexterous Robotic Grasping), a structured framework for language-driven functional dexterous grasping. First, a Knowledge-guided Language Parsing (KLP) module leverages structured knowledge to transform natural-language instructions into semantic sextuples of manipulation constraints. Second, a 3D Gaussian Splatting based hierarchical semantic representation and a Triangular Functional Point Localization (TriLocation) module are developed to localize 3D functional contact keypoints under task-tool topology constraints. Finally, a 3D Keypoint Grasp Matrix Transformation (KGT3D+) module maps the localized keypoints and semantic constraints to executable wrist poses and finger-level joint and force commands. Evaluations on 100 open-vocabulary language instructions, 14 task-tool combinations covering four functional intents, and real-world robotic experiments in representative scenes demonstrate that BLaFA can effectively translate natural-language instructions into task-aligned 3D functional contact structures and corresponding dexterous grasps, thereby enabling language-driven robotic tool use across diverse tasks. The source code will be made publicly available. Code will be publicly available at https://github.com/PopeyePxx/BLaFA.</p></details> | Code will be publicly available at https://github.com/PopeyePxx/BLaFA |

## Gaussian rasterization
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[TRUCO: A Scalable Lock-Free Algorithm for Parallel Contour Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37297v3)** | 2026-11-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Extracting binary contours and their containment hierarchy is a fundamental step in many computer vision pipelines. While the standard Suzuki--Abe algorithm is inherently sequential, existing parallel tracers neither preserve its exact contours nor compute their hierarchy. This paper presents TRUCO (Threaded Raster Unrestricted Contour Ownership), a lock-free parallel algorithm for multi-core CPUs that produces the exact output of Suzuki--Abe. TRUCO relies on two core ideas. First, the image is partitioned into horizontal stripes, and a geometric ownership rule allows each thread to trace complete contours independently across stripe boundaries, avoiding both locks and post-processing merges. Second, contour tracing is decoupled from hierarchy reconstruction, which is performed in a subsequent parallel pass and can be skipped entirely when only the contours are needed. We formally prove that TRUCO is deterministic, data-race-free, and identical in output to Suzuki--Abe regardless of thread count. Across diverse high-resolution datasets-from sparse segmentation masks to dense real-world photographs-TRUCO achieves speedups of 4.7x to 17.4x over the fastest sequential baseline with hierarchy computation, and 2.3x to 17.8x without it.</p></details> |  |
| **[GC-MoE on SM120: Overlapping Expert-Parallel Token Dispatch with Warp-Level FP8xFP4 Block-Scaled MMA on Consumer Blackwell](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16219v1)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Expert-parallel Mixture-of-Experts decoding is latency bound: each layer serializes a token exchange, a grouped GEMM over routed experts, and a combine, while only a few tokens per rank are in flight. Prior GC-MoE fused these phases into one persistent, Green-Context-partitioned CUDA Graph on Hopper (SM90) and datacenter Blackwell (SM100). This paper rebuilds the operator for SM120 (compute capability 12.0, e.g. RTX PRO 5000) over PCIe. SM120 exposes block-scaled tensor cores only through warp-level mma.sync.aligned.kind::mxf8f6f4: no tensor memory, no two-CTA MMA, no cluster multicast. We rebuild the kernel around a swap-AB warp-level block-scaled MMA with FP4 weights in the register A operand and FP8 activations in B, plus SM120-specific mechanisms: scale-byte selection through MMA immediates, a compact-weights path, an adaptive token-tile geometry, an expert-granular streaming dispatch with a batched bulk-pull engine and a 64-bit deduplicating pull cache, full-width BF16 returns drained by resident relay copiers over a position-major arena, and shared experts folded into the compute role. On 8xRTX PRO 5000 over PCIe the operator is 1.522x and 1.387x faster than the FlashInfer CuTe DSL W4A8 MegaMoE and the Cake MegaMoE across 18 decode points (18/18 wins each), and 1.388x faster than Cake with shared experts fused; all 94 numerical cases and 752 per-rank checks pass, at cross-operator cosine similarity of at least 0.9999997.</p></details> | 24 pages, 10 tables, 6 figures |
| **[LILA: Data-Free Structured Pruning of LLMs via Latent Spectral Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11163v2)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structured pruning of large language models (LLMs) has universally required calibration data, gradient computation, or large auxiliary policy networks at pruning time. LILA (\emph{Latent-Informed Layer Analysis}) demonstrates that this dependency is avoidable: each FFN weight matrix is factorized with Non-negative Matrix Factorization (NMF) to obtain a parts-based spectral representation, and neuron importance is scored via the Kolmogorov--Smirnov (KS) distance between singular value distributions of the full and neuron-ablated NMF matrix, a closed-form criterion requiring no calibration corpus, forward pass, or gradient computation. Two data-free matrix variants (Abs, Split) generate pruning masks from weight spectral geometry alone; a calibration-guided variant (Act) is provided as an internal ablation. Evaluation spans 7 model families (up to 70B). Without fine-tuning, data-free LILA surpasses PruneNet (45M-parameter RL policy) by up to 2.12~pp in zero-shot accuracy on LLaMA-2-7B and outperforms WikiText-2-calibrated SliceGPT by up to 6.1~pp across all sparsity levels, confirming data-free performance is not an artifact of omitting activation statistics. After one LoRA RFT epoch (matched WikiText-2 protocol), LILA Act surpasses SliceGPT+RFT by 6.39~pp on average using 98.4\% fewer calibration samples (16 vs.\ 1,024) across LLaMA-2-7B and Phi-2. NTK analysis confirms a $17{\times}$ reduction in functional distortion versus random pruning, providing theoretical grounding for the spectral criterion.</p></details> | 31 Pages |
| **[A Multi-Parent Edge Assembly Crossover Driven Hybrid Genetic Algorithm for the Hamiltonian \textit{p}-Median Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15219v1)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Hamiltonian \textit{p}-median problem seeks to partition the vertices of a graph into exactly $p$ disjoint cycles while minimizing their total cost. This paper presents a hybrid genetic algorithm incorporating a multi-parent edge assembly crossover operator. The crossover combines a base solution with multiple donor solutions in a single recombination operation, thereby expanding the space of available edge exchanges. It uses virtual copies of the base solution to establish edge balance, together with an auxiliary directed graph and a budgeted search to construct valid AB-cycles. Extensive experiments on 215 benchmark instances across four sets show that the proposed algorithm improves the best-known solution values for 34 instances and matches those for another 168. Additional experiments indicate that a moderate number of parents provides beneficial diversity, whereas excessive parent counts, particularly when $p$ is large, lead to excessive population dispersion and hinder convergence.</p></details> |  |
| **[An exact compress-with-another threshold in a binary automaton family](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07044v2)** | 2026-11-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For a state $q$ in a synchronizing automaton, let $μ(q)$ be the minimum length of a word merging $q$ with another initial state. Szykuła asked whether $μ(q)=O(n)$ for strongly connected synchronizing automata and described a binary family attributed to Dżyga, with $n=3p$ states and a conjectured threshold $4n/3$. We specify the boundary transitions omitted from the published piecewise definition and prove that the resulting automata $\mathcal A_p$ satisfy $μ(q_0)=4p=4n/3$ for all $p\geq3$. A shortest word merging $q_0$ with another state is $(ba)^p(ab)^p$. We also construct a reset word of length $3p^2+4p-1$. Consequently, any uniform linear upper bound for $μ(q)$ on binary strongly connected synchronizing automata with corank-one letters has leading constant at least $4/3$.</p></details> | 9 pages, 1 figure. Revised proofs and exposition, corrected boundary transitions, updated references, and added a coauthor |
| **[Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. The dataset comprises 770 RANS simulations of 194 unique wing configurations, sampled across 27 airfoil profile variants and 4 distances from the ground, spanning variations in wing sweep, chord, taper, angle of attack, and tip twist. Six neural network architectures representing distinct paradigms --- convolutional (DoMINO), graph-based (X-MeshGraphNet), transformer-based (AB-UPT, GeoTransolver, GAOT), and JEPA-based (AeroJEPA) --- are evaluated on their ability to predict surface pressure coefficient and wall shear stress distributions, from which integral drag and lift coefficients are recovered. The dataset is intended to be used as a challenging benchmark for surrogate CFD models, due to the uniqueness of the ground effect being absent from other wing and airfoil datasets. The dataset is available for download at https://huggingface.co/datasets/andreacenzato/Ground-Wing</p></details> |  |
| **[Active Learning for Gaussian Process Regression Under Self-Induced Boltzmann Weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10654v2)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We consider the active learning problem where the goal is to learn an unknown function with low prediction error under an unknown Boltzmann distribution induced by the function itself. This self-induced weighting arises naturally in problems such as potential energy surface (PES) modeling in computational chemistry, yet poses unique challenges as the target distribution is unknown and its partition function is intractable. We propose \texttt{AB-SID-iVAR}, a Gaussian Process-based acquisition function that approximates the intractable Bayesian target distribution in closed form while avoiding partition function estimation, and is applicable to both discrete and continuous input domains. We also analyze a Thompson sampling alternative (\texttt{TS-SID-iVAR}) as a higher variance Monte Carlo variant. Despite the unknown target, under mild conditions, we establish that the terminal prediction error vanishes with high probability, and provide a tighter average-case guarantee. We demonstrate consistent improvements over existing approaches in this setting on synthetic benchmarks and real-world PES modeling and drug discovery tasks. Our code is available at https://github.com/TsingQAQ/SIDAL</p></details> |  |

## Gaussian pruning

| **[QuantileGS: Quantile-Based Conformal Bounds on RGB Rendering Errors for Novel View Synthesis with 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21253v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Uncertainty quantification for novel view synthesis with 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) assesses rendering reliability at unseen viewpoints. This reliability depends on the discrepancy between rendered and true colors, which cannot be measured without ground-truth images. We therefore quantify uncertainty in this unknown discrepancy through upper bounds on per-pixel RGB mean absolute error. However, learned bounds may underestimate these errors and overstate rendering reliability, motivating calibration to a prescribed coverage level. In this paper, we propose QuantileGS, a post-hoc framework that combines learned rendering-error quantiles with scale-normalized conformal calibration to produce spatially adaptive error bounds. The framework comprises three phases. Phase 1, Rendering-Error Computation, measures RGB mean absolute error between observed images and predictions from a frozen 3DGS model. Phase 2, Ordered Gaussian Quantile Fields, learns two scalar attributes per Gaussian to predict lower and upper rendering-error quantiles. Phase 3, Scale-Normalized Conformal Calibration, calibrates the upper prediction on held-out images, using the quantile gap to scale the correction. Under the stated assumptions, the resulting bounds provide finite-sample marginal coverage. At a nominal coverage level of 90%, calibration raises mean empirical test coverage from 69.6% to 89.6% on LLFF and from 80.1% to 92.1% on Mip-NeRF 360. On LF, coverage remains conservative at 96.3% before and 96.4% after calibration. Applications to active view selection, Gaussian pruning, and RGB-D simultaneous localization and mapping illustrate how these bounds inform downstream decisions.</p></details> |  |

| **[Optimized Minimal 4D Gaussian Splatting for Efficient Dynamic Scene Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.03857v2)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>4D Gaussian Splatting has emerged as a new paradigm for dynamic scene representation, enabling real-time rendering of scenes with complex motions. However, it faces a major challenge of storage overhead, as millions of Gaussians are required for high-fidelity reconstruction. Compressing explicit 4D Gaussians is challenging because they exhibit heterogeneous redundancy across static and dynamic regions, and their temporal attributes make direct quantization unstable. In this work, we present OMG4 (Optimized Minimal 4D Gaussian Splatting), a framework that constructs a compact set of salient Gaussians capable of faithfully representing 4D Gaussian models. Our method progressively reduces Gaussians in three stages: (1) Gaussian Sampling to identify primitives critical to reconstruction fidelity, (2) Gaussian Pruning to remove redundancies, and (3) Gaussian Merging to fuse primitives with similar characteristics. In addition, we integrate implicit appearance compression and extend Sub-Vector Quantization (SVQ) to 4D representations with a staged quantization scheme, further reducing storage while preserving quality. Extensive experiments on standard benchmark datasets demonstrate that OMG4 achieves a favorable rate-distortion trade-off over recent state-of-the-art methods, reducing model sizes by over 60% while maintaining reconstruction quality. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of OMG4 as a practical framework for compact and high-fidelity 4D scene representation. Our source code is available at https://minshirley.github.io/OMG4/.</p></details> | 23 pages, 10 figures |

| **[Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. ## Gaussian compression
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[TRUCO: A Scalable Lock-Free Algorithm for Parallel Contour Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37297v3)** | 2026-11-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Extracting binary contours and their containment hierarchy is a fundamental step in many computer vision pipelines. While the standard Suzuki--Abe algorithm is inherently sequential, existing parallel tracers neither preserve its exact contours nor compute their hierarchy. This paper presents TRUCO (Threaded Raster Unrestricted Contour Ownership), a lock-free parallel algorithm for multi-core CPUs that produces the exact output of Suzuki--Abe. TRUCO relies on two core ideas. First, the image is partitioned into horizontal stripes, and a geometric ownership rule allows each thread to trace complete contours independently across stripe boundaries, avoiding both locks and post-processing merges. Second, contour tracing is decoupled from hierarchy reconstruction, which is performed in a subsequent parallel pass and can be skipped entirely when only the contours are needed. We formally prove that TRUCO is deterministic, data-race-free, and identical in output to Suzuki--Abe regardless of thread count. Across diverse high-resolution datasets-from sparse segmentation masks to dense real-world photographs-TRUCO achieves speedups of 4.7x to 17.4x over the fastest sequential baseline with hierarchy computation, and 2.3x to 17.8x without it.</p></details> |  |
| **[GC-MoE on SM120: Overlapping Expert-Parallel Token Dispatch with Warp-Level FP8xFP4 Block-Scaled MMA on Consumer Blackwell](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16219v1)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Expert-parallel Mixture-of-Experts decoding is latency bound: each layer serializes a token exchange, a grouped GEMM over routed experts, and a combine, while only a few tokens per rank are in flight. Prior GC-MoE fused these phases into one persistent, Green-Context-partitioned CUDA Graph on Hopper (SM90) and datacenter Blackwell (SM100). This paper rebuilds the operator for SM120 (compute capability 12.0, e.g. RTX PRO 5000) over PCIe. SM120 exposes block-scaled tensor cores only through warp-level mma.sync.aligned.kind::mxf8f6f4: no tensor memory, no two-CTA MMA, no cluster multicast. We rebuild the kernel around a swap-AB warp-level block-scaled MMA with FP4 weights in the register A operand and FP8 activations in B, plus SM120-specific mechanisms: scale-byte selection through MMA immediates, a compact-weights path, an adaptive token-tile geometry, an expert-granular streaming dispatch with a batched bulk-pull engine and a 64-bit deduplicating pull cache, full-width BF16 returns drained by resident relay copiers over a position-major arena, and shared experts folded into the compute role. On 8xRTX PRO 5000 over PCIe the operator is 1.522x and 1.387x faster than the FlashInfer CuTe DSL W4A8 MegaMoE and the Cake MegaMoE across 18 decode points (18/18 wins each), and 1.388x faster than Cake with shared experts fused; all 94 numerical cases and 752 per-rank checks pass, at cross-operator cosine similarity of at least 0.9999997.</p></details> | 24 pages, 10 tables, 6 figures |
| **[LILA: Data-Free Structured Pruning of LLMs via Latent Spectral Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11163v2)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structured pruning of large language models (LLMs) has universally required calibration data, gradient computation, or large auxiliary policy networks at pruning time. LILA (\emph{Latent-Informed Layer Analysis}) demonstrates that this dependency is avoidable: each FFN weight matrix is factorized with Non-negative Matrix Factorization (NMF) to obtain a parts-based spectral representation, and neuron importance is scored via the Kolmogorov--Smirnov (KS) distance between singular value distributions of the full and neuron-ablated NMF matrix, a closed-form criterion requiring no calibration corpus, forward pass, or gradient computation. Two data-free matrix variants (Abs, Split) generate pruning masks from weight spectral geometry alone; a calibration-guided variant (Act) is provided as an internal ablation. Evaluation spans 7 model families (up to 70B). Without fine-tuning, data-free LILA surpasses PruneNet (45M-parameter RL policy) by up to 2.12~pp in zero-shot accuracy on LLaMA-2-7B and outperforms WikiText-2-calibrated SliceGPT by up to 6.1~pp across all sparsity levels, confirming data-free performance is not an artifact of omitting activation statistics. After one LoRA RFT epoch (matched WikiText-2 protocol), LILA Act surpasses SliceGPT+RFT by 6.39~pp on average using 98.4\% fewer calibration samples (16 vs.\ 1,024) across LLaMA-2-7B and Phi-2. NTK analysis confirms a $17{\times}$ reduction in functional distortion versus random pruning, providing theoretical grounding for the spectral criterion.</p></details> | 31 Pages |
| **[A Multi-Parent Edge Assembly Crossover Driven Hybrid Genetic Algorithm for the Hamiltonian \textit{p}-Median Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15219v1)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Hamiltonian \textit{p}-median problem seeks to partition the vertices of a graph into exactly $p$ disjoint cycles while minimizing their total cost. This paper presents a hybrid genetic algorithm incorporating a multi-parent edge assembly crossover operator. The crossover combines a base solution with multiple donor solutions in a single recombination operation, thereby expanding the space of available edge exchanges. It uses virtual copies of the base solution to establish edge balance, together with an auxiliary directed graph and a budgeted search to construct valid AB-cycles. Extensive experiments on 215 benchmark instances across four sets show that the proposed algorithm improves the best-known solution values for 34 instances and matches those for another 168. Additional experiments indicate that a moderate number of parents provides beneficial diversity, whereas excessive parent counts, particularly when $p$ is large, lead to excessive population dispersion and hinder convergence.</p></details> |  |
| **[An exact compress-with-another threshold in a binary automaton family](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07044v2)** | 2026-11-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For a state $q$ in a synchronizing automaton, let $μ(q)$ be the minimum length of a word merging $q$ with another initial state. We specify the boundary transitions omitted from the published piecewise definition and prove that the resulting automata $\mathcal A_p$ satisfy $μ(q_0)=4p=4n/3$ for all $p\geq3$. A shortest word merging $q_0$ with another state is $(ba)^p(ab)^p$. We also construct a reset word of length $3p^2+4p-1$. Consequently, any uniform linear upper bound for $μ(q)$ on binary strongly connected synchronizing automata with corank-one letters has leading constant at least $4/3$.</p></details> | 9 pages, 1 figure. Revised proofs and exposition, corrected boundary transitions, updated references, and added a coauthor |
| **[Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. The dataset comprises 770 RANS simulations of 194 unique wing configurations, sampled across 27 airfoil profile variants and 4 distances from the ground, spanning variations in wing sweep, chord, taper, angle of attack, and tip twist. Six neural network architectures representing distinct paradigms --- convolutional (DoMINO), graph-based (X-MeshGraphNet), transformer-based (AB-UPT, GeoTransolver, GAOT), and JEPA-based (AeroJEPA) --- are evaluated on their ability to predict surface pressure coefficient and wall shear stress distributions, from which integral drag and lift coefficients are recovered. The dataset is intended to be used as a challenging benchmark for surrogate CFD models, due to the uniqueness of the ground effect being absent from other wing and airfoil datasets. The dataset is available for download at https://huggingface.co/datasets/andreacenzato/Ground-Wing</p></details> |  |
| **[Active Learning for Gaussian Process Regression Under Self-Induced Boltzmann Weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10654v2)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We consider the active learning problem where the goal is to learn an unknown function with low prediction error under an unknown Boltzmann distribution induced by the function itself. This self-induced weighting arises naturally in problems such as potential energy surface (PES) modeling in computational chemistry, yet poses unique challenges as the target distribution is unknown and its partition function is intractable. We propose \texttt{AB-SID-iVAR}, a Gaussian Process-based acquisition function that approximates the intractable Bayesian target distribution in closed form while avoiding partition function estimation, and is applicable to both discrete and continuous input domains. We also analyze a Thompson sampling alternative (\texttt{TS-SID-iVAR}) as a higher variance Monte Carlo variant. Despite the unknown target, under mild conditions, we establish that the terminal prediction error vanishes with high probability, and provide a tighter average-case guarantee. We demonstrate consistent improvements over existing approaches in this setting on synthetic benchmarks and real-world PES modeling and drug discovery tasks. Our code is available at https://github.com/TsingQAQ/SIDAL</p></details> |  |

Second, contour tracing is decoupled from hierarchy reconstruction, which is performed in a subsequent parallel pass and can be skipped entirely when only the contours are needed. We formally prove that TRUCO is deterministic, data-race-free, and identical in output to Suzuki--Abe regardless of thread count. Across diverse high-resolution datasets-from sparse segmentation masks to dense real-world photographs-TRUCO achieves speedups of 4.7x to 17.4x over the fastest sequential baseline with hierarchy computation, and 2.3x to 17.8x without it.</p></details> |  |
| **[Random Cut Forests, Games and Diffusion over Approximately Nested Anisotropic Local Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19548v1)** | 2026-11-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Just as the data in a dynamic stream changes, questions asked of the stream also change in intent: what to hold constant, which counterfactuals to consider, and so forth. Energy-based learning has been celebrated for its uniform approach to losses and estimation, but interpretability remains challenging. Tree-based methods, on the other hand, offer high interpretability but often rely on bespoke, disjointed estimators. Neither paradigm readily supports changing questions on streaming data. In this paper we take a step towards combining energy, interpretability, and streaming. Random cut forests retain an evolving geometry of the data. We study this geometry as the basis for diffusion dynamics, prediction, explanation, and games. We show that several existing scores are escape probabilities of random walks on the trees. The induced affinity is realized exactly by a sparse electrical network on each tree, giving a single energy calculus, a Hamiltonian, maintained along one path per update. This connects an estimate, its supporting evidence, and the response to intervention through the same boxes, gaps, masses, and paths. Different questions correspond to different clamp decisions on the energy. Fixing observed values gives harmonic prediction with Gaussian uncertainty. Fixing a partition gives normalized cuts whose cost is attributed exactly to oriented faces. Releasing clamps gives coalition games, in which collusive displacement measures the field response. With noise, the same energy gives diffusion dynamics whose relaxation rates are read from the boxes. Axis-parallel cuts localize at a rate set by affine rather than ambient dimension, and this control passes to the averaged energy. Across a forest the boxes are only approximately nested; common reference observations align the trees. A rotating star with added noise illustrates the measurements.</p></details> |  |
| **[GC-MoE on SM120: Overlapping Expert-Parallel Token Dispatch with Warp-Level FP8xFP4 Block-Scaled MMA on Consumer Blackwell](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16219v1)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Expert-parallel Mixture-of-Experts decoding is latency bound: each layer serializes a token exchange, a grouped GEMM over routed experts, and a combine, while only a few tokens per rank are in flight. Prior GC-MoE fused these phases into one persistent, Green-Context-partitioned CUDA Graph on Hopper (SM90) and datacenter Blackwell (SM100). This paper rebuilds the operator for SM120 (compute capability 12.0, e.g. RTX PRO 5000) over PCIe. SM120 exposes block-scaled tensor cores only through warp-level mma.sync.aligned.kind::mxf8f6f4: no tensor memory, no two-CTA MMA, no cluster multicast. We rebuild the kernel around a swap-AB warp-level block-scaled MMA with FP4 weights in the register A operand and FP8 activations in B, plus SM120-specific mechanisms: scale-byte selection through MMA immediates, a compact-weights path, an adaptive token-tile geometry, an expert-granular streaming dispatch with a batched bulk-pull engine and a 64-bit deduplicating pull cache, full-width BF16 returns drained by resident relay copiers over a position-major arena, and shared experts folded into the compute role. On 8xRTX PRO 5000 over PCIe the operator is 1.522x and 1.387x faster than the FlashInfer CuTe DSL W4A8 MegaMoE and the Cake MegaMoE across 18 decode points (18/18 wins each), and 1.388x faster than Cake with shared experts fused; all 94 numerical cases and 752 per-rank checks pass, at cross-operator cosine similarity of at least 0.9999997.</p></details> | 24 pages, 10 tables, 6 figures |

Two data-free matrix variants (Abs, Split) generate pruning masks from weight spectral geometry alone; a calibration-guided variant (Act) is provided as an internal ablation. Evaluation spans 7 model families (up to 70B). Without fine-tuning, data-free LILA surpasses PruneNet (45M-parameter RL policy) by up to 2.12~pp in zero-shot accuracy on LLaMA-2-7B and outperforms WikiText-2-calibrated SliceGPT by up to 6.1~pp across all sparsity levels, confirming data-free performance is not an artifact of omitting activation statistics. After one LoRA RFT epoch (matched WikiText-2 protocol), LILA Act surpasses SliceGPT+RFT by 6.39~pp on average using 98.4\% fewer calibration samples (16 vs.\ 1,024) across LLaMA-2-7B and Phi-2. This paper presents a hybrid genetic algorithm incorporating a multi-parent edge assembly crossover operator. It uses virtual copies of the base solution to establish edge balance, together with an auxiliary directed graph and a budgeted search to construct valid AB-cycles. Additional experiments indicate that a moderate number of parents provides beneficial diversity, whereas excessive parent counts, particularly when $p$ is large, lead to excessive population dispersion and hinder convergence.</p></details> |  |

A shortest word merging $q_0$ with another state is $(ba)^p(ab)^p$. We also construct a reset word of length $3p^2+4p-1$. | **[Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. The dataset comprises 770 RANS simulations of 194 unique wing configurations, sampled across 27 airfoil profile variants and 4 distances from the ground, spanning variations in wing sweep, chord, taper, angle of attack, and tip twist. Six neural network architectures representing distinct paradigms --- convolutional (DoMINO), graph-based (X-MeshGraphNet), transformer-based (AB-UPT, GeoTransolver, GAOT), and JEPA-based (AeroJEPA) --- are evaluated on their ability to predict surface pressure coefficient and wall shear stress distributions, from which integral drag and lift coefficients are recovered. The dataset is intended to be used as a challenging benchmark for surrogate CFD models, due to the uniqueness of the ground effect being absent from other wing and airfoil datasets. The dataset is available for download at https://huggingface.co/datasets/andreacenzato/Ground-Wing</p></details> |  |
| **[ColNav: Uncertainty-Aware Spacetime Collision Checking with Bounding Volume Hierarchies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11646v1)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Safe navigation for mobile robots in dynamic and uncertain environments requires planning paths that avoid collisions with other moving targets. The key aspect of our approach is encoding the uncertainty in the targets' and the ego robot's positions and velocities into spacetime tubes, in two variants that consume bounded-set and Gaussian uncertainty descriptions, respectively. The tubes are organised in bounding volume hierarchies, so that a single tree traversal covers the entire horizon, rather than testing sampled time instants, and returns a robust geometric certificate together with an estimate of the time to collision. Static and dynamic obstacles share the same representation. Our approach reaches the goal with a 100% success rate under prediction model mismatch, where the best baseline reaches 65%, and maintains up to three times the clearance in dense traffic. ## feed-forward 3D reconstruction
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Refin3R: Geometry-Guided Refinement for Feed-Forward 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23051v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feed-forward 3D reconstruction models predict dense point maps from multiple images in a single pass. Without explicit geometric constraints, however, their predictions are often inconsistent across views and lack fine-grained precision. Recent methods incorporate geometric priors such as camera pose or depth, but use them merely as auxiliary inputs, process each view independently, or refine the geometry only where the priors are available. We present Refin3R, which refines feed-forward point maps with Structure-from-Motion (SfM) points, propagating SfM geometry over the image grid. In its dual-branch architecture, a 2D branch extends SfM geometry beyond its coverage using geometry-aware backbone features, while a 3D branch reasons over the SfM points of all views, capturing multi-view structure that the image grid alone cannot represent. The two branches exchange information bidirectionally to jointly refine each view, yielding dense and accurate point maps. Across ScanNet++, ETH3D, Tanks\&amp;Temples, and out-of-domain human and surgical scenes, Refin3R consistently outperforms feed-forward and geometry-guided methods in point map accuracy while running faster than the strongest geometry-guided baseline.</p></details> |  |
| **[Déjà-Events: Multi-view 3D Geometry with Event Cameras via Sparsity-Aware Adaptive Gating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21302v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Event cameras keep capturing structure where conventional cameras blur or saturate, which makes them attractive for 3D perception in fast or poorly lit scenes. Event datasets with dense 3D ground truth, however, are far smaller and less diverse than the image collections behind feed-forward 3D reconstruction models. We present Déjà-Events, an event-only model that predicts depth, camera pose and pointmaps from multiple views in a single forward pass. Rather than relying on scarce 3D labels, we distil it from Déjà View, a feed-forward RGB model that refines its tokens by repeatedly applying one shared transformer block. On paired RGB and event recordings, the event student learns to reproduce the frozen teacher's final tokens, which requires no depth or pose ground truth and trains only about 1% of its parameters. Because events are dense at edges and moving texture but nearly absent in textureless regions, we further introduce sparsity-aware adaptive gating, which adapts the shared block's gates to events and adjusts them per patch by event density, so that accuracy holds as refinement steps are added. Trained only on simulated data, Déjà-Events achieves the lowest pointmap error among the event-based methods we evaluate, on EventScape and zero-shot on the real MVSEC and DSEC datasets. On DSEC it halves the error of the strongest baseline (0.15 vs. 0.30 AbsRel) with about a tenth of its parameters. It also has the lowest depth error on EventScape and DSEC and the lowest trajectory error on EventScape.</p></details> | 8 pages, 6 figures |
| **[Eco3R: Pinned-Key Inference for Efficient and Streamable Feed-Forward 3D Reconstruction from Long Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18099v2)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feed-forward multi-view transformers such as VGGT recover the cameras and geometry of a video in one pass, but the cost of their cross-frame self-attention grows quadratically with the frame count, and their accuracy drops on long videos. We hypothesize that the keys of a long video can be selected independently of video content, and our experiments on VGGT and $π^3$ support it: a small number of uniformly sampled frames can serve as the keys of every frame. We present Eco3R, a training-free inference framework whose keys form a Pinned Key Set of 31 such frames. A pin pass stores their tokens in a Scene Cache, and the other frames read it in independent batches that reproduce joint inference with these keys exactly, at a constant cost per frame. An Online Stream updates the pinned frames during recording. Six middle global blocks align the frames and form a reusable VGGT module: the Alignment Score reads their attention to flag failures, and Alignment Refocusing reduces them by fine-tuning only these blocks (3.6 GPU hours). Eco3R ($π^3$) matches $π^3$'s success rate on 1,000-frame ScanNet videos while running 13.8 times faster. With refocusing, Eco3R (VGGT) raises VGGT's success rate from 70% to 82% while running 8.5 times faster on 500 frames. Eco3R can reconstruct sequences of up to 4,000 frames on a single A100 GPU, while Eco3R ($π^3$) streams 1,000 frames at 18.6 fps, achieving the lowest online absolute trajectory error.</p></details> | 26 pages, 10 figures. v2: renamed the method from EcoVGGT to Eco3R, added Figure 1, and revised the text |

| **[SpaceMCP: Vision Language Model Spatial Reasoning via Model Context Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15680v1)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite impressive advances in large language models (LLM), vision-language models (VLM) still exhibit limited spatial reasoning capabilities. | **[RIGOR: Rig-Informed Geometry for Omnidirectional Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13504v3)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent developments in feed-forward 3D reconstruction resulted in models which can recover dense scene representations and camera motion solely from an image stream. | **[Camera3R: Feed-Forward 3D Reconstruction from Heterogeneous Camera Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13453v1)** | 2026-11-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feed-forward 3D reconstruction (3R) from unconstrained multi-view images has recently emerged as a prominent research direction in 3D computer vision. When these models fail, there is no mechanism to correct their mistakes. Experiments show that Click3R reduces reconstruction error with as few as a single correspondence click, improving performance on both standard and our curated benchmarks. Our results highlight that sparse human interaction provides an effective and practical mechanism for correcting geometric reconstruction errors.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026. | **[3R-Adapter: Retrieval, Rewiring, and Refinement for Efficient Adaptation of 3D Reconstruction Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11964v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feed-forward 3D reconstruction models have demonstrated strong generalization under large-scale pre-training, yet they struggle in long-tail scenarios such as low-light reconstruction, joint human-scene reconstruction, and test-time adaptation. Extensive experiments on low-light reconstruction, joint human-scene reconstruction, and test-time adaptation demonstrate that our approach significantly improves robustness over existing PEFT methods while maintaining efficient lightweight adaptation.
